`docs/COMPATIBILITY.md`:

```md
# Compatibility

UbuRoot needs an **arm64** phone with a root manager (Magisk, KernelSU or APatch) that
gives **full root**: uid 0 with every capability, no seccomp filter, no `no_new_privs`,
and `su --mount-master` reaching the init mount namespace. The app checks this before
installing and refuses a restricted root (for example a KernelSU App Profile with fewer
capabilities) with the reason. Everything else (kernel features, hardware) is reported
by the doctor, and what's missing is explained rather than assumed.

## Tested devices

Add a row when you test a device (see the checklist below), and link the redacted doctor
report (a gist, or the issue/PR where you posted it).

| Device | SoC | ROM | Android | Kernel | Root manager | UbuRoot | Result | Doctor report |
|--------|-----|-----|---------|--------|--------------|---------|--------|---------------|
| Samsung Galaxy S9 (SM-G9600, `starqltechn`) | Snapdragon 845 / Adreno 630 | LineageOS 19.1 | 12 | 4.9 (vendor, not GKI) | Magisk 31.1 | 0.3.0 | Everything in the checklist passes. Not tested: recovery install, FBE boot wait (`/data` is unencrypted) | maintainer's runs, summarized in `CLAUDE.md` |

Not tested on any device yet: KernelSU, APatch, Android 13 or newer, GKI kernels,
Mali GPUs, encrypted (FBE) `/data` with boot start.

## Device testing checklist

Modelled on Kali NetHunter's. Each item names the automated check where there is one.
Commands run from a computer with `adb` and the repository checked out.

### 1. Root and preflight
- [ ] Root manager and version, Android version, ROM noted.
- [ ] Doctor (Settings → Diagnostics → Run diagnostics) shows `full root: [ok]` and
      `mount namespace: [ok] global`. Save the redacted report for the table.
- [ ] The app asked for root once and the preflight passed.

### 2. Install
- [ ] Online install: discover → download → SHA256 → extract → setup → terminal.
- [ ] Turn the screen off, or switch apps, during the download: the install
      continues (wake lock; the screen stays on while the app is open).
- [ ] Kill the app during extraction, then reopen it: it resumes, or starts over cleanly.
- [ ] Offline install (*Advanced* in the install form): a `.tar.gz` or `.tar.xz` from
      `cdimage.ubuntu.com` picked from storage, with and without its SHA256.
- [ ] An existing install from an older UbuRoot is migrated on start (doctor:
      `integration schema: [ok] 1`).

### 3. Automated suites
- [ ] `tests/device/run.sh -s SERIAL`: mounts and propagation, wrappers, the user's
      sudo/network/storage, a sabotaged reset, umount back to the baseline, root
      quality and doctor. It needs a finished install (the app's is fine).
- [ ] `tests/device/session-test.sh` inside an app terminal (usage in its header): tty
      and job control, sudo, DNS/apt/HTTPS/ICMP, storage groups, read-only Android
      partitions, binder tools, package database checks.
- [ ] Optional: `tests/device/advanced-test.sh` (developer and network tools).

### 4. Terminal
- [ ] Tabs, root session, extra keys (sticky CTRL), links, themes, font size.
- [ ] Hardware keyboard: Ctrl+Shift+C/V/T.
- [ ] Closing a tab ends its processes (`ps` in another tab).
- [ ] *Stop all* closes every session and unmounts in a few seconds.

### 5. Android integration
- [ ] `uburoot-notify`, `uburoot-toast`, `uburoot-vibrate`, `uburoot-clip`,
      `uburoot-open FILE|URL`, as the user and as root, with the app in the background.
- [ ] Share a file and a text to *Open in UbuRoot*.
- [ ] `am`, `pm`, `settings get`, `dumpsys battery` through `sudo`.

### 6. Boot module
- [ ] Install the APK as a module from the root manager, enable *Start at boot*, put a
      script in `/etc/uburoot-boot.d/`, reboot: it ran (`/data/local/uburoot/boot.log`).
- [ ] On an encrypted phone, it waits for the first unlock.

### 7. Host integrity (the safety rule)
- [ ] After *Stop all*, `adb shell su -c 'cat /proc/1/mountinfo'` matches the snapshot
      taken before the first session (the device suite checks this automatically).
- [ ] Android's Wi-Fi, Bluetooth, camera and audio still work afterwards.

### 8. Report
- [ ] Add a row to the table above (or open an issue with the doctor report and the
      checklist results).

```

`docs/HARDWARE.md`:

```md
# Hardware access

`HARDWARE_ACCESS=1` (Settings → *Hardware access*, behind a warning) gives Ubuntu the
phone's hardware where the kernel allows it. It is off by default. It adds **no
mounts**: `/dev` and `/sys` are already the phone's own (see the mount design in
`CLAUDE.md`). What it adds:

- `uburoot-hw` inside Ubuntu (and `hardware.sh` on the Android side, used by the app):
  per-class reports, group grants, permission rules for hot-plugged devices, the Wi-Fi
  takeover, CPU limits, a wake lock, LEDs and bus nodes;
- the Hardware screen (terminal menu → *Hardware*).

Every change goes to the same change log as `uburoot-kernel`
(`/data/local/uburoot/state/changes`, `/run/uburoot/state/changes` inside Ubuntu).
*Stop all*, *Revert changes* (Kernel or Hardware screen) and a reboot undo it.

## Safety floor

No option changes these:

- thermal trip points, charger and battery settings and voltages are read-only
  (`uburoot-hw set` only accepts `/sys/class/leds/NAME/brightness|trigger`);
- bus nodes whose device name looks like a PMIC, charger, regulator, fuel gauge or
  battery are refused by `uburoot-hw allow`;
- the modem is report-only (no AT or NV writes);
- `cpu online` never takes `cpu0` offline, and frequencies must come from
  `scaling_available_frequencies`.

## Routes

| Route | Meaning |
|-------|---------|
| direct | the kernel interface is free: Ubuntu uses the device node or sysfs file itself |
| bridge | Android owns the device through a HAL; Ubuntu gets it through the app (Phase 3b) |
| takeover | Android is asked to stop using it, then Ubuntu uses the kernel interface |
| read | report only |
| none | no kernel interface on this phone |

## Classes

`uburoot-hw list` prints every class, `uburoot-hw info CLASS` its nodes, their owners and
which processes hold them open.

| Class | Route | Group (grant) | Notes |
|-------|-------|---------------|-------|
| gpu | direct | `aid_graphics` 1003 | `/dev/kgsl-3d0`, `/dev/dri/*`, `fb*`. A GPU driver for Ubuntu (Mesa freedreno on Adreno) is Phase 4 |
| input | direct | `aid_input` 1004 | `/dev/input/event*`. Reading them sees every touch and key, Android's too |
| uinput | direct | `aid_uhid` 3011 | `/dev/uinput`, `/dev/uhid`: virtual keyboards/mice |
| audio | bridge | `aid_audio` 1005 | `/dev/snd/*` is held by Android's audio HAL |
| camera | bridge | `aid_camera` 1006 | `video*`/`media*`/`v4l-subdev*` are the ISP's, held by the camera HAL |
| usb | direct | `aid_usb` 1018 | `/dev/bus/usb/*` (OTG host). ueventd gives them `root:usb 0660` |
| serial | direct | `dialout` 20 | `ttyACM*`, `ttyUSB*`: ueventd has no rule (root 0600), so a grant adds a **permission rule** |
| hidraw | direct | `plugdev` 46 | `hidraw*`: same, a permission rule |
| wifi | takeover | — | see below |
| bluetooth | none / bridge | — | see below |
| nfc | none | `aid_nfc` 1027 | see below |
| sensors | bridge | — | Android's sensor HAL (Phase 3b) |
| leds | direct | — | `uburoot-hw set /sys/class/leds/NAME/brightness V` (recorded) |
| cpu | direct | — | `uburoot-hw cpu governor|min|max|online` (recorded) |
| power, thermal, modem | read | — | battery, thermal zones and trips, modem nodes |
| buses | read | `uburoot-bus` | `i2c-*`, `spidev*`, `gpiochip*`: `uburoot-hw allow NODE` opens one (recorded) |

A grant is group membership (`usermod -aG`), so it applies to new sessions. Revoking
the last user of a rule class drops its rule.

**Permission rules.** Android's ueventd creates the nodes; the S9's `ueventd.rc` has no
rule for `ttyACM*`, `ttyUSB*` or `hidraw*`, so they are `root 0600`. A rule class's
nodes get its group and `0660` (each node's original owner and mode is recorded). A
watcher inside Ubuntu (`uburoot-hw watch`, every 2 s, started by `mount.sh` and by a
grant) does the same for devices plugged in later. `umount.sh` stops it, and the revert
puts every node back.

## Wi-Fi

Without a takeover Android keeps `wlan0` and `wpa_supplicant`; `iw dev`, `iw phy` and
`iw scan` (as root) still work beside it.

`uburoot-hw takeover wifi` records Android's state (`takeover wifi WIFI:SCAN`), then runs
`cmd wifi set-scan-always-available disabled` and `cmd wifi set-wifi-enabled disabled`
and waits for Android's Wi-Fi to stop. `wlan0` is then Ubuntu's: `ip link set wlan0 up`,
`iw dev wlan0 scan`, `wpa_supplicant`, `hostapd` (the S9's phy supports AP mode).
`uburoot-hw release wifi` (or *Stop all*) stops Ubuntu's `wpa_supplicant`, `hostapd`,
`dhclient`, `dhcpcd` and `iwd` (only processes whose executable is Ubuntu's, never
Android's `/vendor` binaries) and restores both settings.

S9 (2026-11-16): after the two `cmd wifi` calls `wlan0` went down, Android's
`/vendor/bin/hw/wpa_supplicant` exited and `phy0` stayed; Ubuntu's `ip link set wlan0 up`
and `iw dev wlan0 scan` saw the nearby networks. Supported interface modes: IBSS,
managed, AP, P2P. **No monitor mode** on this phone.

Tested on the S9 (2026-11-16) from the CLI and from the Hardware screen: Ubuntu's own
`wpa_supplicant` ran on `wlan0` after the takeover, and *Give back* stopped it; Android
then started its own again and Wi-Fi came back.

## Bluetooth and NFC (S9)

- **Bluetooth:** the chip (Broadcom BCM4361) sits on `ttyHS0` (the BT UART) and is
  driven by Android's Bluetooth stack in userspace. The kernel has no `BT_HCIUART`,
  `BT_HCIBTUSB` or `BT_HCIVHCI`, so BlueZ has no HCI device to use and a takeover is
  impossible. A bridge would have to go through Android's Bluetooth APIs (later). A USB
  Bluetooth dongle needs `BT_HCIBTUSB`, which is missing too.
- **NFC:** `/dev/sec-nfc` (Samsung driver) exists, but `CONFIG_NFC` is off: there is no
  kernel NFC subsystem for `neard`/libnfc. Report only.

## Other S9 findings (2026-11-16)

- GPU/display: `/dev/kgsl-3d0` `system:system 0666`, `/dev/dri/*` `root:graphics 0666`,
  `fb0` `root:graphics 0660`.
- Audio: 116 `/dev/snd/*` nodes, `system:audio`. Camera: `video*`, `media*`,
  `v4l-subdev*` `system:camera`. No UVC (`MEDIA_USB_SUPPORT` is off), so a USB webcam
  won't work.
- USB host drivers in the kernel: `USB_ACM`, `USB_SERIAL_FTDI_SIO`, `USB_SERIAL_PL2303`
  (no CP210x, no CH341), `SND_USB_AUDIO`, CDC/NCM/AX88179 Ethernet, `HIDRAW`, `UHID`,
  `INPUT_UINPUT`. `I2C_CHARDEV`, `SPI_SPIDEV`, `GPIO_SYSFS` are on; IIO is on but has no
  devices.
- CPU: `policy0` (cpus 0–3, 576–1766 MHz) and `policy4` (cpus 4–7, 826–2803 MHz),
  `schedutil`. Android's power HAL (`vendor.qti.hardware.perf@2.0-service`) and
  `thermal-engine` may change limits back.
- Battery: Samsung's driver exposes many `batt_tune_*` and voltage files: read-only by
  design.
- `rfkill` lists the BCM4361 Bluetooth, `phy0` and `brcmfmac-wifi`.

## Change log types

Besides `sysctl`, `selinux` and `module` (see `KERNEL.md`):

| Line | Revert |
|------|--------|
| `file PATH VALUE` | writes VALUE back (skipped if the file is gone: an offline CPU, an unplugged device) |
| `node PATH UID:GID:MODE` | `chown` + `chmod` back (skipped if the node is gone) |
| `wakelock NAME -` | writes NAME to `/sys/power/wake_unlock` |
| `takeover wifi W:S` | stops Ubuntu's Wi-Fi daemons, restores scanning and Wi-Fi |

```

`docs/KERNEL.md`:

```md
# Kernel access

UbuRoot can give root inside Ubuntu the phone kernel's own interfaces. It's off by
default. Turn it on in **Settings → Kernel → Kernel access** (a warning explains what it
allows), then **Repair mounts**. Users without `sudo` get nothing new.

Everything here works on the running kernel only. UbuRoot never flashes or changes the
kernel image (that is the separate kernel manager, a later phase).

## What you get

With `KERNEL_ACCESS=1`, `mount.sh` adds, inside Ubuntu:

| Path in Ubuntu | What | How |
|---|---|---|
| `/sys/kernel/config` | configfs (USB gadget, …) | bind of the host's (Android 12 mounts it at `/config`) |
| `/sys/kernel/debug` | debugfs | bind of the host's, else a new instance |
| `/sys/kernel/tracing` | tracefs (ftrace) | bind, else new |
| `/sys/fs/bpf` | BPF filesystem (netd's pinned maps are there) | bind, else new |
| `/sys/fs/cgroup` | cgroup v2 | bind |
| `/dev/{cpuset,cpuctl,memcg,stune,blkio,freezer}` | cgroup v1 controllers | bind, at the same paths as Android |
| `/sys/fs/pstore` | previous boot's console / panic log | bind, else new |
| `/sys/fs/selinux` | selinuxfs | bind |
| `/sys/kernel/security`, `/sys/fs/fuse/connections`, `/proc/sys/fs/binfmt_misc` | securityfs, fusectl, binfmt_misc | bind, else new, only if the kernel has it |
| `/usr/lib/modules/$(uname -r)/uburoot/…` | the ROM's kernel modules (`/vendor/lib/modules`, `/vendor_dlkm`, `/system_dlkm`, `/odm`, `/lib/modules`) | read-only binds, then `depmod` into the rootfs |
| `/usr/local/sbin/uburoot-kernel`, `uburoot-kmod`, `uburoot-kbuild` | the tools below | |

A bind is the host's instance: what you change there changes Android too (a USB gadget,
a cgroup, a tracing buffer). Every bind is a slave mount, so unmounting it in Ubuntu
never unmounts it on the host. *Stop all* and *Repair mounts* leave the host's mount
table exactly as it was (the device suite and the Docker suite check this).

## `uburoot-kernel`

Run it as root (`sudo uburoot-kernel …`). Every change is **recorded** with the value it
replaced, in `/data/local/uburoot/state/changes`, and undone by:

- `uburoot-kernel revert` (or *Revert changes* on the Kernel screen),
- *Stop all*,
- a reboot (nothing is written to persistent storage; a log from an earlier boot is
  discarded).

Only the first change of each setting is recorded, so a revert goes back to the value
from before UbuRoot touched it. Changes are undone newest first.

| Command | Does |
|---|---|
| `status` | access on/off, SELinux mode, the restrict sysctls, recorded changes |
| `sysctl NAME [VALUE]` | read, or set and record, `/proc/sys/NAME` |
| `devmode on\|off` | `kernel.kptr_restrict` down to 1 (root can read kernel addresses) |
| `selinux [enforcing\|permissive]` | switch SELinux for the **whole phone** (asks first; `-y` skips) |
| `module list` | loaded modules; `*` = loaded through UbuRoot |
| `module load FILE.ko\|NAME [ARGS]` | `insmod` a file, or `modprobe` a ROM module by name (inside Ubuntu); recorded |
| `module unload NAME [--force]` | `rmmod` |
| `lastlog` | the previous boot's kernel log (pstore, `/proc/last_kmsg`) |
| `revert` | undo every recorded change |

`uburoot-kmod ARGS` is `uburoot-kernel module ARGS`.

Changes made **by hand** (`echo … > /proc/sys/…`, plain `insmod`, `setenforce`) are not
recorded and are not undone by *Stop all*. They still go away at the next reboot.

## The Kernel screen

Menu → **Kernel**: the current state, developer mode, the SELinux switch (with the
strongest warning), recorded changes with *Revert changes*, loaded modules (unload for
the ones loaded through UbuRoot), the live kernel log (`dmesg -w` in a root tab), the
previous boot's log, and the kernel part of the doctor report.

## Building modules: `uburoot-kbuild`

```
sudo apt install build-essential bc flex bison libssl-dev libelf-dev kmod
uburoot-kbuild check            # what this kernel accepts
uburoot-kbuild prepare ~/linux  # the exact source of the running kernel
uburoot-kbuild build ~/mymod
sudo uburoot-kmod load ~/mymod/mymod.ko
```

`check` names the kernel type (GKI, which is the Android Common Kernel branch
`androidNN-X.Y`, or a vendor kernel) and the vermagic a module needs. It also says
whether signing, symbol versions or GKI's symbol list will stop an out-of-tree module.
`prepare` takes the config from `/proc/config.gz` and sets `LOCALVERSION`, so that
`make kernelrelease` equals `uname -r`. It refuses otherwise.

## SELinux

Under Magisk, KernelSU and APatch, root's own SELinux domain is already permissive, so
SELinux doesn't block Ubuntu's root even when the phone is enforcing:

- Magisk: `magisk_rules()` calls `permissive([proc])` for `u:r:magisk:s0`
  (`native/src/sepolicy/rules.rs`, commit `70d233cae2`, 2026-11-10). On the S9 the
  kernel logs `permissive=1` on the denials that domain hits.
- KernelSU: `ksu_permissive(db, KERNEL_SU_DOMAIN)` plus `allow ALL ALL ALL` for
  `u:r:ksu:s0` (`kernel/selinux/rules.c` lines 92 and 103, commit `019a3c0103`,
  2026-11-06).
- APatch: its su runs as `u:r:magisk:s0` by default (`ALL_ALLOW_SCONTEXT_MAGISK` in
  KernelPatch `kernel/patch/include/uapi/scdefs.h`) and applies Magisk's policy.

So `selinux permissive` is almost never what you need: it turns SELinux off for every
app and service on the phone. It exists for the rare case where a tool talks to an
Android service that refuses it. It is always temporary.

## Limits (measured on the S9: kernel 4.9, vendor, LineageOS 19.1)

- **Module signing is enforced** (`CONFIG_MODULE_SIG_FORCE=y`). An unsigned module fails
  with `Required key not available`, and `uburoot-kmod` explains why. Vendors don't
  publish the signing key, so out-of-tree modules need a kernel you build yourself.
  The S9's ROM ships no loadable modules either.
- **bpftool** can't list programs or maps on kernels older than 4.13 (no
  `BPF_OBJ_GET_INFO_BY_FD` or ID iteration). Loading BPF programs and the pinned maps in
  `/sys/fs/bpf` still work. bpftrace and CO-RE need BTF (`CONFIG_DEBUG_INFO_BTF`), which
  this kernel lacks.
- **trace-cmd** works (tracefs). **simpleperf** (Android's, as a wrapper) and **perf**
  work as root (`perf_event_paranoid` is 3 with `security.perf_harden=1`, which only
  restricts non-root users).
- **kptr_restrict** can be above 2 on some kernels (the S9 booted with 4, a vendor
  extension). The kernel only accepts 0–2 from a write, so a revert of 4 writes 2,
  which hides addresses from everyone, root included, as 4 did. Android's init also
  writes it: `security.lower_kptr_restrict=1` sets 0, and `=0` sets 2
  (`/system/etc/init/hw/init.rc`). `simpleperf record` does this while it runs.
- **Android's `simpleperf` relaxes perf for the whole phone and leaves it so.** After it
  ran as root, `security.perf_harden` stayed 0 and `perf_event_paranoid` read -1 (boot:
  1 and 3). That is a property, not something `uburoot-kernel` records. Undo it with
  `sudo android setprop security.perf_harden 1` (init then writes 3), or reboot.
  Ubuntu's own `perf` doesn't touch it.
- cgroup v1 controllers are at `/dev/*` as on Android, not at `/sys/fs/cgroup/<name>`.

## Root manager extensions

UbuRoot loads modules with the kernel's `insmod`/`init_module` only. The managers'
own mechanisms are not used, but here is what they do (sources checked 2026-11-16):

- **KernelSU** `ksud insmod MODULE [PARAMS]` resolves the module's undefined symbols
  through KernelSU (or `/proc/kallsyms`) and calls `init_module(2)`
  (`userspace/ksud/src/debug.rs`). Also `ksud sepolicy`, `ksud feature`, profiles.
- **APatch** `apd insmod` is "load a kernel module without version check": it resolves
  symbols from `/proc/kallsyms` (setting `kptr_restrict` to 1 meanwhile), which skips
  the vermagic and CRC checks, then calls `init_module(2)` (`apd/src/insmod.rs`). As far
  as their code shows, both still reach the kernel's own signature check.
  **KPM** (KernelPatch modules) are a different format, loaded by KernelPatch's own
  loader (`kp_load_module_path` in `lkm/kpm/kpm.c`) through a supercall that needs the
  superkey. They are managed from the APatch app.
- **Magisk** has no kernel module loader. `magiskpolicy` patches SELinux rules live.

These are manager-specific (and the superkey must stay with its owner), so UbuRoot
documents them rather than wrapping them.

```

`docs/NETHUNTER_PARITY.md`:

```md
# NetHunter parity

What Kali NetHunter offers for installing and managing its chroot, and what UbuRoot has or
plans instead. UbuRoot is a general-purpose Ubuntu for rooted, de-Googled phones (F-Droid),
not a pentest distribution: it provides the platform, and users install tools with `apt`.

**Edition:** UbuRoot is **root-only**. It requires a root that really is root (full
capabilities, no seccomp, init mount namespace), and it goes beyond NetHunter in three
goals (see `prompt.md`): **full kernel access**, **full hardware access** and **Linux GUI
apps**. The kernel and hardware powers are opt-in toggles (`KERNEL_ACCESS`,
`HARDWARE_ACCESS`) behind warning screens, every change is recorded and reverted by *Stop
all* / *Revert changes*, and a hardware safety floor (thermal, charger, voltages, block
devices, modem NV) never moves. In NetHunter terms it covers *Lite* (any rooted phone,
stock kernel) and reaches into *Full* through the stock kernel's own interfaces and a
kernel manager for user-provided kernels.

Sources (read 2026-11-16): kali.org/docs/nethunter/ (updated 2026-06-23), and these pages
under it: installing-nethunter, nethunter-chroot-manager, nethunter-kex-manager,
nethunter-components, nethunter-home-screen, nethunter-kali-services,
nethunter-custom-commands, nethunter-settings, nethunter-kernel, nethunter-modules,
nethunter-audio, nethunter-usbarsenal, nethunter-mac-changer, nethunter-rootless,
testing-checklist, nethunter-terminal. Several pages are mostly screenshots. Where the text
doesn't say something (e.g. which VNC server KeX uses), the table doesn't claim it.

Status: **done** (in a release), **partial**, **planned**, **out** (out of scope).

## Measured on the test phone (S9, LineageOS 19.1, kernel 4.9.337, Magisk 31.1)

- `su -M`, on the host and as root inside the chroot: uid 0, context `u:r:magisk:s0`,
  `CapEff`/`CapBnd` `0000003fffffffff` (all 38 capabilities of a 4.9 kernel), `Seccomp: 0`.
  SELinux is enforcing; the `magisk` domain is what lets root through.
- `/proc/config.gz` is present (6145 lines). It has `MODULES USER_NS PID_NS NET_NS
  OVERLAY_FS TUN CONFIGFS_FS USB_CONFIGFS USB_CONFIGFS_F_HID USB_CONFIGFS_NCM
  USB_CONFIGFS_MASS_STORAGE USB_CONFIGFS_ACM CFG80211 WIREGUARD BPF_SYSCALL IP_NF_IPTABLES
  BLK_DEV_LOOP USB_SERIAL USB_ACM BT F2FS_FS`. It lacks `BINFMT_MISC`, `MAC80211`, `CAN`,
  `NF_TABLES`, `USB_CONFIGFS_RNDIS`, `SQUASHFS` and `KVM`. No modules are loaded
  (`/proc/modules` is empty).
- configfs is mounted at **`/config`** (old Android path, with `usb_gadget`), debugfs at
  `/sys/kernel/debug`, tracefs at `/sys/kernel/tracing`.
- **Gap:** inside the chroot, `/sys/kernel/config`, `/sys/kernel/debug`,
  `/sys/kernel/tracing` and `/sys/fs/bpf` are **empty**. The chroot's fresh `sysfs` mount
  has none of the host's submounts. Ubuntu's root can't see configfs, debugfs, tracefs or
  bpffs today, and there is no `/lib/modules/$(uname -r)` for `modprobe`.

| HDMI / cast, Termux:X11 | — | Part of the Phase 4 display research | 4 | planned | |
| — (beyond NetHunter) **Linux GUI apps** | — | App mode (one Linux app per Android window where possible), GPU acceleration (Turnip + Zink on Adreno; others researched), input/IME/clipboard, `.desktop` apps pinned as launcher shortcuts; acceptance: Wireshark live capture, GIMP, a browser, gqrx, Ghidra, an editor, glmark2/vkcube | 4 | planned | |
| **Hardware** | | | | | |
| USB Arsenal: USB function selector (RNDIS…), image mounter (mass storage) | — | configfs gadget: NCM/RNDIS where the kernel has them (S9: NCM, no RNDIS), ACM serial, mass storage of an image, generic HID keyboard/mouse as an input device; Android's gadget config saved and restored | 3 | planned | HID *payloads* stay out. |
| OTG wireless / SDR / Bluetooth adapters | `/dev` is bound, root sees it | `/dev/bus/usb`, `ttyUSB*`/`ttyACM*` for the user through the device access model (`uburoot-hw grant`) | 3 | planned | |
| MAC changer | Root can run `ip link set address` (shared netns) | A tool definition (Phase 5) with a warning that changing the MAC under Android's Wi-Fi drops the connection | 5 | planned | No dedicated screen. |
| — (beyond NetHunter) **Full hardware access** | — | `HARDWARE_ACCESS=1`: per class direct / bridge / takeover, documented in `docs/HARDWARE.md`: CPU/power/thermal (read thermal, never disable trips), GPU/display, input, audio, camera (Camera2 → V4L2/socket), sensors and location (→ IIO-like/gpsd), Wi-Fi (takeover + monitor where the phy supports it), Bluetooth, NFC, USB, buses read-only by default; `uburoot-hw` CLI and a Hardware screen | 3 | 3a done (classes, grants, permission rules, Wi-Fi takeover, CPU/LEDs/wake lock/buses, Hardware screen); 3b planned (audio, camera, sensors/location bridges, USB gadget) | Modem: report only, never AT/NV writes. S9: no Bluetooth takeover (no HCI UART/USB/VHCI driver), no kernel NFC, no UVC, no monitor mode. |
| — (beyond NetHunter) **Full kernel access** | `KERNEL_ACCESS`, `uburoot-kernel`/`-kmod`/`-kbuild`, Kernel screen, change log reverted by Stop all | See the *Root and kernel* rows: kernel filesystems, modules, logs, tracing/eBPF, tunables, SELinux, out-of-tree modules, root-manager extensions, a Kernel screen | 2 | done | |
| **Offensive modules** | | | | | |
| HID keyboard attacks, DuckHunter, BadUSB MITM | — | — | — | out | Non-goal (HID/BadUSB payloads). |
| Mana, EvilTwin, Wifipumpkin, WPS attacks, Pineapple connector | — | — | — | out | Non-goal (rogue AP / captive portal / WPS). |
| Metasploit Payload Generator, SET | — | — | — | out | Non-goal (payload generators, social engineering). |
| Searchsploit, Nmap scan screens | — | Generic tool definitions (Phase 5); nmap bundled as an example | 5 | planned | |
| Bluetooth Arsenal, CARsenal, Wardriving | — | No dedicated screens; hardware access (Phase 3) and tool definitions (Phase 5) cover the platform side | 3, 5 | planned (platform) | Attack front-ends stay out. |
| Wi-Fi injection (custom kernel) | Doctor lists Wi-Fi phys and monitor support (S9: none) | Doctor lists monitor-capable phys; Wi-Fi takeover with monitor mode where the phy supports it; kernel build guide for drivers | 1, 3, 10 | done (report); planned | The S9's internal driver has no monitor mode. |
| **Store, devices, testing** | | | | | |
| NetHunter Store | — | — | — | out | Non-goal (app store). F-Droid and GitHub releases. |
| Supported device list (CI-generated kernel/image reports) | `docs/COMPATIBILITY.md` (S9 row) | `docs/COMPATIBILITY.md`: device, ROM, Android, root manager + version, result, doctor bundle | 1 | done | |
| Testing checklist (boot, apps, kernel version, HID, Metasploit, external Wi-Fi) | Checklist in `docs/COMPATIBILITY.md`; device suite runs the doctor | Checklist in `docs/` built from the suites, with doctor output | 1 | done | |
| Porting (kernel builder, patches) | — | Kernel build guide (docs + optional CI recipe) | 10 | planned | Not in the app. |

## Phase list (`prompt.md`, with the changes proposed here)

0. Gap analysis (this file). **0b.** Relicense to GPL-3.0-or-later (v0.3.0).
1. **Install hardening and diagnostics**: doctor with kernel report and hardware inventory
   (and who owns each device), rootfs schema + migrations, wake lock / mirror / offline
   install, `COMPATIBILITY.md` + testing checklist. **Plus** the full-root gate in the
   preflight (the user's "require full kernel access"): a restricted root is refused with
   the reason, instead of failing later.
2. **Full kernel access** (`KERNEL_ACCESS`): kernel filesystems, modules, logs,
   tracing/eBPF, tunables, SELinux, out-of-tree modules, root-manager extensions, Kernel
   screen. The binds measured as missing above land here, behind the toggle (not always
   on, as an earlier draft of this file proposed).
3. **Full hardware access** (`HARDWARE_ACCESS`), `docs/HARDWARE.md`, Hardware screen.
4. **Linux GUI apps**: research gate (`docs/GUI.md`) first, then app/desktop modes, GPU,
   audio, input, launcher shortcuts, acceptance apps.
5. **Native tool front-ends** (declarative).
6. **Chroot manager**: backup, restore, verify, move, profiles, release upgrade.
7. **Services and custom commands.**
8. **Status home screen**, plus *Android shell* / *Android root shell* session types.
9. **Architectures and Android versions.**
10. **Kernel manager** (backup, flash, keep root, restore), each device run approved first.
11. **Updates, settings backup, documentation.**
- Optional: rootless decision document. Given that the user requires full kernel access,
  the expected outcome is "a separate app, not this one". Kept short.
```

`docs/ROOT_IMPLEMENTATIONS.md`:

```md
# Root implementations: Magisk, KernelSU, APatch

UbuRoot supports the three main root managers. This page records how they differ in
the details that matter for a chroot: how `su` is provided, how to reach the global
mount namespace, where busybox lives and how modules install. Everything here was
**checked in each project's source code** (2026-11), not guessed. If you find that a
newer version behaves differently, please open an issue.

Only Magisk has been tested on a real device so far (Magisk 31.1, LineageOS 19.1 /
Android 12). The KernelSU and APatch paths follow from their source code and are
untested.

## Why the mount namespace matters

UbuRoot mounts `/proc`, `/dev`, `/sdcard`, `/data`, `/system`... under the rootfs. Every
terminal session and every script must see the **same** mounts. If one `su` call runs in
a private mount namespace, its mounts are invisible to the others, and an unmount there
can't release mounts made elsewhere. So every root call from UbuRoot has to run in the
**global (init) mount namespace**.

## Comparison

| | Magisk | KernelSU | APatch |
|-|--------|----------|--------|
| `su -v` prints | `<version>:MAGISKSU` | `<version>:KernelSU` | `<version>:APatch` |
| Global-namespace flag | `-M` / `--mount-master` (also `-mm`) | `-M` / `--mount-master` | `-M` / `--mount-master` |
| Where it is implemented | `native/src/core/su/su.cpp` (`"mount-master"`, `'M'`; `-mm` is rewritten) | `userspace/ksud/src/su.rs` (`optflag("M", "mount-master")` → `switch_mnt_ns(1)`) | `apd/src/apd.rs` (`optflag("M", "mount-master")` → `switch_mnt_ns(1)`) |
| How `su` is provided | a real `su` binary served by `magiskd` | the kernel's sucompat redirects `/system/bin/su` to `ksud`, which sees `argv[0] = su` (`cli.rs`) | KernelPatch redirects `su`/`kp` to `apd` (`cli.rs`, `arg0.ends_with("su")`) |
| Global namespace without the flag | per-app "Mount Namespace Mode" in Magisk settings (default: inherit) | per-app profile `namespace` (0 inherit, 1 global, 2 individual) | global if `/data/adb/.global_namespace_enable` contains `1` |
| `su -c` with several arguments | joins the arguments after `-c` | takes only the value of `-c` | takes only the value of `-c` |
| Busybox | `/data/adb/magisk/busybox` | `/data/adb/ksu/bin/busybox` | `/data/adb/ap/bin/busybox` |
| Module install from the manager | yes | yes | yes |
| Module flash from custom recovery | yes (`update-binary` → `install_module`) | no (per its docs) | no (per its docs) |

## What UbuRoot does with this

- **Always `--mount-master`.** All three accept it, so there's no per-implementation
  branching. libsu builds it with `Shell.FLAG_MOUNT_MASTER` (see `UbuRootApp`).
- **Never trusts the flag blindly.** libsu silently falls back to plain `su`, and then to
  `sh`, when `su --mount-master` fails. Preflight compares `/proc/self/ns/mnt` with init's
  (`/proc/1/ns/mnt`) and refuses to continue when they differ.
- **The command is one string**: terminal sessions run
  `su --mount-master -c "/data/local/uburoot/bin/enter.sh <user>"`, because ksud and apd
  only read the value of `-c`.
- **Detection** (`common.sh`, reported by `rootinfo.sh`): the `su -v` suffix first, then
  the `/data/adb/{magisk,ksu,ap}` directories as a fallback.
- **Busybox** comes from the root manager's path above. Without one, the scripts fall back
  to toybox/plain commands; that path is untested, and every root manager ships a
  busybox.

## Module (the APK is also a module)

The release APK carries `module.prop`, `customize.sh`, `service.sh` and
`META-INF/com/google/android/{update-binary,updater-script}` at its root.

- **From the manager** (all three): `customize.sh` uses `SKIPUNZIP=1` and extracts only
  `module.prop` and `service.sh`, never the app's dex or libraries. It keeps a copy of
  the APK and installs it with `pm install -r` right away (`BOOTMODE=true`). With
  `SKIPUNZIP=1`, Magisk doesn't copy `module.prop` by itself (see `install_module` in its
  `util_functions.sh`), which is why `customize.sh` extracts it.
- **From TWRP** (Magisk only): `update-binary` sources
  `/data/adb/magisk/util_functions.sh` and calls `install_module`, the same thing
  Magisk's own `module_installer.sh` does. `service.sh` then installs the app after
  `sys.boot_completed`. On KernelSU/APatch it prints that recovery isn't supported and
  exits 1.

## SELinux domain and kernel extensions

All three put root in a **permissive** SELinux domain: Magisk `u:r:magisk:s0`
(`permissive([proc])` in `native/src/sepolicy/rules.rs`), KernelSU `u:r:ksu:s0`
(`ksu_permissive` in `kernel/selinux/rules.c`), and APatch uses `u:r:magisk:s0` with
Magisk's policy. So SELinux doesn't block Ubuntu's root even on an enforcing phone.
KernelSU (`ksud insmod`) and APatch (`apd insmod`) can load modules without the
vermagic/CRC checks; APatch also has KPM, KernelPatch's own module format. UbuRoot
uses plain `insmod` only. Details and source references are in [KERNEL.md](KERNEL.md).

## Known quirks

- **Magisk sometimes rejects without a prompt.** On the test device, Magisk 31.1 logged
  `su: request rejected (<uid>)` and never showed its dialog, even with default settings.
  The fix was allowing the app by hand in Magisk's superuser list. The cause is unknown.
- **No controlling terminal (Magisk).** Magisk's `su` gives the root process the
  terminal's pts but calls `setsid()` without making it the controlling terminal. Bash
  then has no job control, and Ctrl+C goes to the wrong process group. `enter.sh` runs
  util-linux `setsid --ctty` to take the tty over. Whether ksud/apd behave the same is
  not known yet; the fix is harmless if they don't.
```

`fastlane/metadata/android/en-US/full_description.txt`:

```txt
<p>UbuRoot installs the official Ubuntu Base image and runs it in a chroot, using the root you already have (Magisk, KernelSU or APatch). It is not proot emulation: programs run natively, at full speed, with real sudo, apt, compilers, servers and network tools.</p>

<p><b>Features</b></p>
<ul>
<li>Finds the newest Ubuntu LTS, downloads it with resume support and verifies it against Ubuntu's SHA256SUMS</li>
<li>Sets up DNS, apt sources, sudo and your user</li>
<li>Real terminal (PTY) with job control, 256 colors and UTF-8, in tabs that keep running in the background</li>
<li>Extra keys, themes, pinch to zoom, clickable links and hardware keyboard shortcuts</li>
<li>Your shared storage (/sdcard) and Android's own commands (pm, am, cmd, settings, dumpsys...) inside Ubuntu</li>
<li>uburoot-notify, uburoot-toast, uburoot-vibrate, uburoot-clip and uburoot-open (xdg-open): talk to Android from Ubuntu</li>
<li>"Open in UbuRoot" share target: a terminal in the shared file's folder</li>
<li>The APK is also a Magisk/KernelSU/APatch module, which can start Ubuntu and your services at boot</li>
<li>No Google services, no analytics. Network access only to Ubuntu's servers and whatever you run yourself</li>
</ul>

<p><b>Requirements:</b> an arm64 phone with Android 8.0+, root (Magisk, KernelSU or APatch) and about 3 GB free.</p>

<p><b>Warning:</b> root inside Ubuntu is root on your phone. Never delete the Ubuntu folder by hand while it is mounted, because your real /data and /sdcard are mounted inside it. Use the reset script instead.</p>

<p>Ubuntu is a trademark of Canonical Ltd. This project is not affiliated with or endorsed by Canonical.</p>
```

`gradlew`:

```
#!/bin/sh

#
# Copyright © 2015 the original authors.
#
# Licensed under the Apache License, Version 2.0 (the "License");
# you may not use this file except in compliance with the License.
# You may obtain a copy of the License at
#
#      https://www.apache.org/licenses/LICENSE-2.0
#
# Unless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing, software
# distributed under the License is distributed on an "AS IS" BASIS,
# WITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY KIND, either express or implied.
# See the License for the specific language governing permissions and
# limitations under the License.
#
# SPDX-License-Identifier: Apache-2.0
#

##############################################################################
#
#   Gradle start up script for POSIX generated by Gradle.
#
#   Important for running:
#
#   (1) You need a POSIX-compliant shell to run this script. If your /bin/sh is
#       noncompliant, but you have some other compliant shell such as ksh or
#       bash, then to run this script, type that shell name before the whole
#       command line, like:
#
#           ksh Gradle
#
#       Busybox and similar reduced shells will NOT work, because this script
#       requires all of these POSIX shell features:
#         * functions;
#         * expansions «$var», «${var}», «${var:-default}», «${var+SET}»,
#           «${var#prefix}», «${var%suffix}», and «$( cmd )»;
#         * compound commands having a testable exit status, especially «case»;
#         * various built-in commands including «command», «set», and «ulimit».
#
#   Important for patching:
#
#   (2) This script targets any POSIX shell, so it avoids extensions provided
#       by Bash, Ksh, etc; in particular arrays are avoided.
#
#       The "traditional" practice of packing multiple parameters into a
#       space-separated string is a well documented source of bugs and security
#       problems, so this is (mostly) avoided, by progressively accumulating
#       options in "$@", and eventually passing that to Java.
#
#       Where the inherited environment variables (DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS, JAVA_OPTS,
#       and GRADLE_OPTS) rely on word-splitting, this is performed explicitly;
#       see the in-line comments for details.
#
#       There are tweaks for specific operating systems such as AIX, CygWin,
#       Darwin, MinGW, and NonStop.
#
#   (3) This script is generated from the Groovy template
#       https://github.com/gradle/gradle/blob/2d6327017519d23b96af35865dc997fcb544fb40/platforms/jvm/plugins-application/src/main/resources/org/gradle/api/internal/plugins/unixStartScript.txt
#       within the Gradle project.
#
#       You can find Gradle at https://github.com/gradle/gradle/.
#
##############################################################################

# Attempt to set APP_HOME

# Resolve links: $0 may be a link
app_path=$0

# Need this for daisy-chained symlinks.
while
    APP_HOME=${app_path%"${app_path##*/}"}  # leaves a trailing /; empty if no leading path
    [ -h "$app_path" ]
do
    ls=$( ls -ld "$app_path" )
    link=${ls#*' -> '}
    case $link in             #(
      /*)   app_path=$link ;; #(
      *)    app_path=$APP_HOME$link ;;
    esac
done

# This is normally unused
# shellcheck disable=SC2034
APP_BASE_NAME=${0##*/}
# Discard cd standard output in case $CDPATH is set (https://github.com/gradle/gradle/issues/25036)
APP_HOME=$( cd -P "${APP_HOME:-./}" > /dev/null && printf '%s\n' "$PWD" ) || exit

# Use the maximum available, or set MAX_FD != -1 to use that value.
MAX_FD=maximum

warn () {
    echo "$*"
} >&2

die () {
    echo
    echo "$*"
    echo
    exit 1
} >&2

# OS specific support (must be 'true' or 'false').
cygwin=false
msys=false
darwin=false
nonstop=false
case "$( uname )" in                #(
  CYGWIN* )         cygwin=true  ;; #(
  Darwin* )         darwin=true  ;; #(
  MSYS* | MINGW* )  msys=true    ;; #(
  NONSTOP* )        nonstop=true ;;
esac



# Determine the Java command to use to start the JVM.
if [ -n "$JAVA_HOME" ] ; then
    if [ -x "$JAVA_HOME/jre/sh/java" ] ; then
        # IBM's JDK on AIX uses strange locations for the executables
        JAVACMD=$JAVA_HOME/jre/sh/java
    else
        JAVACMD=$JAVA_HOME/bin/java
    fi
    if [ ! -x "$JAVACMD" ] ; then
        die "ERROR: JAVA_HOME is set to an invalid directory: $JAVA_HOME

Please set the JAVA_HOME variable in your environment to match the
location of your Java installation."
    fi
else
    JAVACMD=java
    if ! command -v java >/dev/null 2>&1
    then
        die "ERROR: JAVA_HOME is not set and no 'java' command could be found in your PATH.

Please set the JAVA_HOME variable in your environment to match the
location of your Java installation."
    fi
fi

# Increase the maximum file descriptors if we can.
if ! "$cygwin" && ! "$darwin" && ! "$nonstop" ; then
    case $MAX_FD in #(
      max*)
        # In POSIX sh, ulimit -H is undefined. That's why the result is checked to see if it worked.
        # shellcheck disable=SC2039,SC3045
        MAX_FD=$( ulimit -H -n ) ||
            warn "Could not query maximum file descriptor limit"
    esac
    case $MAX_FD in  #(
      '' | soft) :;; #(
      *)
        # In POSIX sh, ulimit -n is undefined. That's why the result is checked to see if it worked.
        # shellcheck disable=SC2039,SC3045
        ulimit -n "$MAX_FD" ||
            warn "Could not set maximum file descriptor limit to $MAX_FD"
    esac
fi

# Collect all arguments for the java command, stacking in reverse order:
#   * args from the command line
#   * the main class name
#   * -classpath
#   * -D...appname settings
#   * --module-path (only if needed)
#   * DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS, JAVA_OPTS, and GRADLE_OPTS environment variables.

# For Cygwin or MSYS, switch paths to Windows format before running java
if "$cygwin" || "$msys" ; then
    APP_HOME=$( cygpath --path --mixed "$APP_HOME" )

    JAVACMD=$( cygpath --unix "$JAVACMD" )

    # Now convert the arguments - kludge to limit ourselves to /bin/sh
    for arg do
        if
            case $arg in                                #(
              -*)   false ;;                            # don't mess with options #(
              /?*)  t=${arg#/} t=/${t%%/*}              # looks like a POSIX filepath
                    [ -e "$t" ] ;;                      #(
              *)    false ;;
            esac
        then
            arg=$( cygpath --path --ignore --mixed "$arg" )
        fi
        # Roll the args list around exactly as many times as the number of
        # args, so each arg winds up back in the position where it started, but
        # possibly modified.
        #
        # NB: a `for` loop captures its iteration list before it begins, so
        # changing the positional parameters here affects neither the number of
        # iterations, nor the values presented in `arg`.
        shift                   # remove old arg
        set -- "$@" "$arg"      # push replacement arg
    done
fi


# Add default JVM options here. You can also use JAVA_OPTS and GRADLE_OPTS to pass JVM options to this script.
DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS='"-Xmx64m" "-Xms64m"'

# Collect all arguments for the java command:
#   * DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS, JAVA_OPTS, and optsEnvironmentVar are not allowed to contain shell fragments,
#     and any embedded shellness will be escaped.
#   * For example: A user cannot expect ${Hostname} to be expanded, as it is an environment variable and will be
#     treated as '${Hostname}' itself on the command line.

set -- \
        "-Dorg.gradle.appname=$APP_BASE_NAME" \
        -jar "$APP_HOME/gradle/wrapper/gradle-wrapper.jar" \
        "$@"

# Stop when "xargs" is not available.
if ! command -v xargs >/dev/null 2>&1
then
    die "xargs is not available"
fi

# Use "xargs" to parse quoted args.
#
# With -n1 it outputs one arg per line, with the quotes and backslashes removed.
#
# In Bash we could simply go:
#
#   readarray ARGS < <( xargs -n1 <<<"$var" ) &&
#   set -- "${ARGS[@]}" "$@"
#
# but POSIX shell has neither arrays nor command substitution, so instead we
# post-process each arg (as a line of input to sed) to backslash-escape any
# character that might be a shell metacharacter, then use eval to reverse
# that process (while maintaining the separation between arguments), and wrap
# the whole thing up as a single "set" statement.
#
# This will of course break if any of these variables contains a newline or
# an unmatched quote.
#

eval "set -- $(
        printf '%s\n' "$DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS $JAVA_OPTS $GRADLE_OPTS" |
        xargs -n1 |
        sed ' s~[^-[:alnum:]+,./:=@_]~\\&~g; ' |
        tr '\n' ' '
    )" '"$@"'

exec "$JAVACMD" "$@"

```

```bat
@rem
@rem SPDX-License-Identifier: Apache-2.0
@rem

@if "%DEBUG%"=="" @echo off
@rem ##########################################################################
@rem
@rem  Gradle startup script for Windows
@rem
@rem ##########################################################################

@rem Set local scope for the variables with windows NT shell
if "%OS%"=="Windows_NT" setlocal

set DIRNAME=%~dp0
if "%DIRNAME%"=="" set DIRNAME=.
@rem This is normally unused
set APP_BASE_NAME=%~n0
set APP_HOME=%DIRNAME%

@rem Resolve any "." and ".." in APP_HOME to make it shorter.
for %%i in ("%APP_HOME%") do set APP_HOME=%%~fi

@rem Add default JVM options here. You can also use JAVA_OPTS and GRADLE_OPTS to pass JVM options to this script.
set DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS="-Xmx64m" "-Xms64m"

@rem Find java.exe
if defined JAVA_HOME goto findJavaFromJavaHome

set JAVA_EXE=java.exe
%JAVA_EXE% -version >NUL 2>&1
if %ERRORLEVEL% equ 0 goto execute

echo. 1>&2
echo ERROR: JAVA_HOME is not set and no 'java' command could be found in your PATH. 1>&2
echo. 1>&2
echo Please set the JAVA_HOME variable in your environment to match the 1>&2
echo location of your Java installation. 1>&2

goto fail

:findJavaFromJavaHome
set JAVA_HOME=%JAVA_HOME:"=%
set JAVA_EXE=%JAVA_HOME%/bin/java.exe

if exist "%JAVA_EXE%" goto execute

echo. 1>&2
echo ERROR: JAVA_HOME is set to an invalid directory: %JAVA_HOME% 1>&2
echo. 1>&2
echo Please set the JAVA_HOME variable in your environment to match the 1>&2
echo location of your Java installation. 1>&2

goto fail

:execute
@rem Setup the command line



@rem Execute Gradle
"%JAVA_EXE%" %DEFAULT_JVM_OPTS% %JAVA_OPTS% %GRADLE_OPTS% "-Dorg.gradle.appname=%APP_BASE_NAME%" -jar "%APP_HOME%\gradle\wrapper\gradle-wrapper.jar" %*

:end
@rem End local scope for the variables with windows NT shell
if %ERRORLEVEL% equ 0 goto mainEnd

:fail
rem Set variable GRADLE_EXIT_CONSOLE if you need the _script_ return code instead of
rem the _cmd.exe /c_ return code!
set EXIT_CODE=%ERRORLEVEL%
if %EXIT_CODE% equ 0 set EXIT_CODE=1
if not ""=="%GRADLE_EXIT_CONSOLE%" exit %EXIT_CODE%
exit /b %EXIT_CODE%

:mainEnd
if "%OS%"=="Windows_NT" endlocal

:omega

```

`prompt.md`:

```md
# UbuRoot — next milestone: NetHunter-level platform, full kernel and hardware access, Linux GUI apps

## Context
You are working on UbuRoot (`com.reinanbr.uburoot`), an Android app that installs and runs
an Ubuntu Base rootfs in a chroot using root (Magisk / KernelSU / APatch), with a real PTY
terminal and deep Android integration. Phases 1–5 of `prompt.md` are done (v0.2.1).

**Read `CLAUDE.md` in full before anything else.** It is the source of truth for
architecture, paths, mount design, the critical safety rule, root-manager differences and
everything already verified on the device. Do not re-derive or contradict it; extend it.

The reference for this milestone is Kali NetHunter's installation and management layer:
- https://www.kali.org/docs/nethunter/installing-nethunter/
- https://www.kali.org/docs/nethunter/ (editions, components)
- https://www.kali.org/docs/nethunter/nethunter-chroot-manager/
- https://www.kali.org/docs/nethunter/nethunter-kex-manager/
- https://www.kali.org/docs/nethunter/nethunter-components/

NetHunter offers: several install channels (pre-built per device, generic installers per
architecture, recovery zip, Magisk module), editions by capability (Rootless / Lite /
Full), minimal and full chroots, a chroot manager (install, backup, restore, remove), a
services manager with run-at-boot, custom commands in the launcher, a desktop (KeX over
VNC/HDMI/cast) with audio, native Android front-ends for tools (Nmap Scan, Searchsploit,
MAC Changer...), a status home screen (interfaces, HID status), kernel and module
management, USB gadget control, settings backup, an update channel, a supported device
list and a porting/testing checklist.

## Goal
Make UbuRoot the most complete Linux-on-Android platform for rooted phones: NetHunter's
level of installation, lifecycle management and diagnostics, and beyond it in three
primary goals.

1. **Full kernel access.** From inside Ubuntu, root can do everything the kernel allows on
   this device: kernel filesystems (debugfs, tracefs, bpffs, configfs, cgroups, pstore...),
   modules, kernel logs, tracing and eBPF, tunables, out-of-tree modules built against the
   running kernel, the root manager's kernel extension points, and backing up, flashing
   and restoring kernels.
2. **Full hardware access.** Every piece of hardware the kernel exposes is reachable from
   Ubuntu with normal Linux tools (CPU/GPU, display, input, audio, camera, sensors, Wi-Fi,
   Bluetooth, NFC, USB host/gadget, LEDs, battery, buses). Where an Android HAL/daemon owns
   the device, UbuRoot offers either a **bridge** (Android API → Linux interface) or a
   **takeover** (stop the Android owner, hand the device to Ubuntu, restore afterwards).
3. **Linux GUI apps on the phone.** Wireshark, GIMP, gqrx, Ghidra, IDEs and any other
   X11/Wayland program run in windows on the phone (and on an external display), with GPU
   acceleration where the hardware allows, audio, touch/keyboard/mouse input and launcher
   shortcuts per app. On top of that, **native Android front-ends** for command-line tools,
   defined declaratively so the community can add them without Kotlin.

Where the kernel, the hardware or Android blocks something (module signature enforcement,
GKI symbol protection, SELinux, missing BTF, a HAL-only device, no open GPU driver...),
detect it, explain it in the app and document the workaround. Never pretend it works.

## Non-goals (do not implement)
- Offensive modules: HID/BadUSB payloads, DuckHunter, rogue AP / captive portal, WPS
  attacks, payload generators. Users install security tools with `apt`; UbuRoot provides
  the platform, the access and the GUI, not the attacks.
- An app store, or hosting our own rootfs tarballs. The only rootfs source stays
  Canonical (`cdimage.ubuntu.com`) or a file the user picks.
- systemd as PID 1.
- Writing to modem NV/EFS, IMEI, or calibration partitions. The modem is report-only.

## Constraints (inherited, non-negotiable)
- CRITICAL SAFETY RULE from `CLAUDE.md`: every recursive deletion under `UBU_BASE` goes
  through `ubu_safe_rm`. Every new feature that touches the rootfs or adds mounts (backup,
  restore, move, upgrade, remove, kernel filesystems, device binds, X11 sockets) must come
  with a test that sabotages the unmount and proves the abort path
  (`tests/test_reset_guard.sh` style + the Docker suite).
- System logic in POSIX `sh` scripts under `app/src/main/assets/uburoot/bin/`, testable via
  `adb shell su --mount-master -c ...` without the app; `shellcheck -s sh` clean; no
  bashisms (mksh/busybox/toybox).
- Always `su --mount-master`, command as one string.
- No Google/proprietary dependencies, no analytics, no new network endpoints except
  Canonical mirrors, GitHub releases (only where stated below) and what the user configures.
- **Licensing:** from Phase 0b on, UbuRoot is **GPL-3.0-or-later**. GPLv3 code (e.g.
  Termux:X11) can now be embedded; Apache-2.0 code (Termux terminal libs, libsu) stays
  compatible with GPLv3. GPL-2.0-only code is **not** compatible with GPLv3: check the
  license of anything you embed and record every third-party component in `NOTICE`.
- UI strings in `values/strings.xml` (English) **and** `values-pt-rBR/strings.xml`.
- **Opt-in power, always reversible.** *Kernel access* (`KERNEL_ACCESS`) and *Hardware
  access* (`HARDWARE_ACCESS`) are toggles in `uburoot.conf`, each behind a warning screen
  that says plainly that root in Ubuntu then controls the phone's kernel/hardware. Every
  action that changes kernel or device state (tunable, module, SELinux mode, CPU governor,
  stopped Android daemon, USB gadget config, takeover) records the original state in
  `/data/local/uburoot/state/` and is reverted by *Stop all*, by a *Revert changes* button
  and, where possible, is naturally lost at reboot. Nothing persists across reboots unless
  the user makes it a boot script.
- **Hardware safety floor** (no toggle disables it): never disable thermal shutdown or
  the charger's safety limits, never raise voltages, never write block devices except in
  the Phase 10 flash/restore flow (verified backup of the exact partition + typed
  confirmation word), never write modem NV.
- Never invent flags, APIs, URLs, device paths or library versions: verify at the source
  (kernel, AOSP, Mesa, project repos) and record the evidence (file, line, date) in
  `CLAUDE.md`.
- Everything in English: code, comments, commits, docs.

## Phase 0 — gap analysis (no code)
1. Read the NetHunter pages above and the current UbuRoot code.
2. Write `docs/NETHUNTER_PARITY.md`: one table, one row per NetHunter capability, with
   columns *NetHunter*, *UbuRoot today*, *Planned UbuRoot equivalent*, *Phase*, *Out of
   scope (why)*. Add rows for the three primary goals that go beyond NetHunter.
3. Propose changes to the phase list below if the analysis justifies them (order, split,
   drop). **Stop and show me the table and the final phase list before Phase 1.**

## Phase 0b — relicense to GPL-3.0-or-later (do this right after Phase 0 is approved)
Context: every commit so far is by the maintainer (reinanbr, with Claude as co-author),
so he can relicense alone. Verify that first (`git log --format='%an %ae'`, merged PRs
from other people, `Co-Authored-By` trailers); if anyone else contributed code, stop and
tell me who.
1. Replace `LICENSE` with the verbatim GPLv3 text from gnu.org.
2. `NOTICE`: UbuRoot copyright + GPL-3.0-or-later; keep the third-party section (Termux
   `terminal-view`/`terminal-emulator` and libsu are Apache-2.0, whose NOTICE terms still
   apply) and add each new component as it is embedded.
3. SPDX headers (`SPDX-License-Identifier: GPL-3.0-or-later`) in our own Kotlin, shell
   and Gradle files. Never touch third-party files (`gradlew`, `gradlew.bat`, the Gradle
   wrapper keep their own Apache-2.0 headers).
4. Update every place that says Apache: README (badge, License section, FAQ if needed),
   `CONTRIBUTING.md` (contributions are accepted under GPL-3.0-or-later, inbound =
   outbound, no CLA), `CLAUDE.md`, `prompt.md`, `tools/fdroid/com.reinanbr.uburoot.yml`
   (`License: GPL-3.0-or-later`), fastlane text if it mentions the license, an *About /
   Licenses* screen in the app if there is one (add it if not: app license + third-party
   licenses).
5. `CHANGELOG.md`: under Unreleased → *Changed*: relicensed from Apache-2.0 to
   GPL-3.0-or-later; releases up to and including v0.2.1 remain available under
   Apache-2.0. Ship it as the next minor version (v0.3.0).
6. `grep -rni apache` at the end must only hit third-party notices and Gradle files.

## Phases (ranked by value/dependency; stop at the end of each and show the result)

### Phase 1 — install hardening and diagnostics
1. `uburoot-doctor` (script + Settings button): root manager/version, mount namespace,
   SELinux mode and the root shell's context, Android/kernel version, FBE state, free
   space, mount table health, iptables backend, DNS, apt reachability.
   - **Kernel report** from `/proc/config.gz` or probing: namespaces, overlayfs, tun,
     binfmt_misc, configfs/USB gadget, HID gadget, CAN, wireguard, GKI or not
     (`uname -r`), `CONFIG_MODULES`, module signature enforcement, `vermagic`/modversions
     of vendor modules, BTF (`/sys/kernel/btf/vmlinux`), kprobes/uprobes,
     `kptr_restrict`, `dmesg_restrict`, `perf_event_paranoid`, `unprivileged_bpf_disabled`.
   - **Hardware inventory**: SoC, CPU clusters/governors, GPU and its device node
     (`/dev/kgsl-3d0`, `/dev/mali0`, DRM nodes), display/DRM/fbdev, input devices, ALSA
     cards, V4L2 nodes, IIO sensors, Wi-Fi phys and supported modes (`iw phy`), Bluetooth
     HCI, NFC node, USB controllers and gadget UDC, LEDs, power supplies, thermal zones,
     I²C/SPI/GPIO/serial nodes, and **who owns each device** (which Android
     process holds it open, from `/proc/*/fd`).
   - Output: human-readable plus a redacted bundle (no IPs, MACs, serials, IMEI or user
     names unless the user opts in), shareable from the app, and an issue template field
     that asks for it.
2. Rootfs versioning: `/etc/uburoot-release` (schema version + install date + Ubuntu
   release). `setup.sh` gets numbered, idempotent migrations so a new app version can
   upgrade an old rootfs's integration files safely. Test old → new in Docker.
3. Install robustness: hold a wake lock and keep the screen on during download/extract
   (NetHunter warns Android kills the installer otherwise); mirror choice for
   `ports.ubuntu.com`; **offline install from a local `.tar.gz` / `.tar.xz`** picked with
   SAF, with optional SHA256.
4. `docs/COMPATIBILITY.md`: device table (device, ROM, Android, root manager + version,
   result, doctor bundle link) and a device testing checklist modelled on NetHunter's.

### Phase 2 — full kernel access (runtime)
Today `mount.sh` mounts only a fresh `proc` and `sysfs`, so `/sys/kernel/debug`,
`/sys/kernel/tracing`, `/sys/fs/bpf`, `/sys/kernel/config` and `/sys/fs/cgroup` are empty
inside Ubuntu. With `KERNEL_ACCESS=1`:
1. **Kernel filesystems.** Where Android already mounted one (cgroup hierarchies, bpffs
   with netd's pinned maps, configfs with the USB gadget, tracefs, pstore), **bind the
   host mount** (made `rslave`, like the other binds) so Ubuntu sees the same objects;
   only mount a new instance where the host has none (debugfs, binfmt_misc, securityfs,
   fusectl). Same paths as on a normal Linux. `umount.sh` handles them in the existing
   private/detach/deepest-first order, and `ubu_safe_rm` gains markers for them. Docker
   suite: mount/umount with the host table identical afterwards.
2. **Modules.** Make `/lib/modules/$(uname -r)` work inside Ubuntu: bind the module
   directories that exist on the device (`/vendor/lib/modules`, `/vendor_dlkm`,
   `/system_dlkm`, others found in research) read-only and generate `modules.dep` with
   `depmod` into a writable overlay dir in the rootfs (never write to vendor). Then
   `lsmod`, `modinfo`, `modprobe`, `insmod`, `rmmod` work as usual. `uburoot-kmod`
   wraps load/unload with a confirmation and records what it loaded for revert.
3. **Kernel logs.** `dmesg`, `dmesg -w`, `/dev/kmsg`, and the previous boot's log from
   pstore (`/sys/fs/pstore`) or `last_kmsg`; a log viewer in the app.
4. **Tracing and eBPF.** ftrace/`trace-cmd` over tracefs; `bpftool`, `bpftrace` and bcc
   where BTF or matching headers exist (report clearly when they don't); `perf` built for
   the running kernel, or Android's `simpleperf` through the existing wrapper mechanism.
5. **Kernel tunables (developer mode).** One-tap relax/revert, with originals recorded:
   `kptr_restrict`, `dmesg_restrict`, `perf_event_paranoid` (Android drives it through
   the `security.perf_harden` property: verify), sysctls under `/proc/sys`. Prefer
   granting access to Ubuntu root only over relaxing it globally for every app.
6. **SELinux.** Show the mode and the root shell's context; a temporary
   enforcing→permissive switch (`setenforce 0`) with the strongest warning in the app,
   auto-revert on *Stop all*, and a note that it weakens every app on the phone. Before
   offering it, find out what actually fails under enforcing for Ubuntu root (Magisk's
   `magisk` domain, KernelSU's and APatch's domains) and fix those cases with targeted
   policy (`magiskpolicy`/`ksud sepolicy`/APatch equivalent) instead, if they exist.
7. **Out-of-tree modules.** `uburoot-kbuild`: prepare a build tree for the running kernel
   (`/proc/config.gz` + the exact source: Android Common Kernel tag derived from
   `uname -r` for GKI, OEM GPL source for vendor kernels), `make modules_prepare`, build
   a module with Ubuntu's `gcc`/`clang`. Document and detect the loading limits:
   `vermagic`, modversions CRCs, `CONFIG_MODULE_SIG_FORCE`, GKI protected symbols.
8. **Root-manager kernel extensions.** Research and expose what exists: APatch
   KernelPatch modules (KPM), KernelSU LKM/GKI mode features, anything Magisk offers.
   Verify in source; record differences in `docs/ROOT_IMPLEMENTATIONS.md`.
9. A *Kernel* screen: capability report, loaded modules, tunables with current/original
   values, SELinux, *Revert changes*.

### Phase 3 — full hardware access
With `HARDWARE_ACCESS=1`. For each class below, research the device node, the Android
owner and the conflicts, then pick the route: **direct** (the kernel interface is free),
**bridge** (an Android API feeds a Linux interface) or **takeover** (stop the owner via
`svc`/`cmd`/`stop <service>`, hand the device over, restore on stop). Document the
result per class in `docs/HARDWARE.md` and show it in a *Hardware* screen.

1. **Device access model.** Ubuntu groups mapped to the Android AIDs/device nodes
   (`audio`, `video`, `input`, `plugdev`, `dialout`, `render`, `bluetooth`...), udev-like
   rules applied by a script (no systemd-udevd), so the user can opt into each class
   without becoming root. `uburoot-hw` CLI: `list`, `info CLASS`, `grant CLASS USER`,
   `takeover CLASS`, `release CLASS`, `status`.
2. **CPU, power and thermal.** cpufreq governors/limits, core online/offline, cpusets,
   thermal zones (read; never disable shutdown trips), battery and charger status,
   charge limit where the driver exposes it, wakelocks and suspend control. Recorded and
   reverted like kernel tunables.
3. **GPU and display.** Expose the GPU node to Ubuntu (`/dev/kgsl-3d0` for Adreno,
   `/dev/mali0`/DRM for others), DRM/KMS and fbdev read access, brightness. Used by
   Phase 4 for acceleration.
4. **Input.** `/dev/input/event*` and `/dev/uinput` (already used in tests) for evdev
   tools, virtual keyboards/mice/gamepads, and external USB/Bluetooth input in Ubuntu.
5. **Audio.** Route A (bridge, default): PulseAudio/PipeWire in Ubuntu with an output and
   input module that reaches Android audio (AAudio/OpenSL through a small native helper
   in the app, or a socket to the app). Route B (takeover): ALSA/tinyalsa on `/dev/snd`
   with audioserver stopped. Pick by what works on the test device.
6. **Camera.** Bridge: Camera2 in the app → frames to Ubuntu as a V4L2 device
   (v4l2loopback if the kernel has or can load it) or an MJPEG/raw socket, so `ffmpeg`,
   OpenCV and GUI apps can use it. Direct V4L2 only where the vendor driver allows it.
7. **Sensors and location.** Bridge: SensorManager → IIO-like files or a socket/D-Bus
   service in Ubuntu (`uburoot-sensors`); LocationManager → `gpsd` feed. Direct IIO where
   sensors are on the kernel's IIO bus.
8. **Wi-Fi.** `iw`/nl80211 diagnostics in place; takeover mode stops Android Wi-Fi and
   runs `wpa_supplicant`/`hostapd` in Ubuntu; monitor mode where the phy supports it
   (internal or USB adapters, with the firmware path the kernel actually searches).
9. **Bluetooth.** Research whether BlueZ can get an HCI socket with Android's stack
   disabled (vendor HCI transport, `/dev/ttyHS*`, hci_uart, USB dongles), and implement
   takeover only if it works; otherwise USB Bluetooth dongles in Ubuntu.
10. **NFC.** Report the node; takeover with libnfc/nfcpy only if the controller is
    accessible; otherwise USB readers (e.g. ACR122U) in Ubuntu.
11. **USB.** Host: `/dev/bus/usb` and serial (`ttyUSB*`, `ttyACM*`) for the user (SDR
    dongles, microcontrollers, adapters). Gadget via configfs: NCM/RNDIS (phone as USB
    network to a laptop), ACM serial, mass storage of an image file, generic HID
    keyboard/mouse as an input device; Android's own gadget config saved and restored.
12. **Buses and misc.** I²C, SPI, GPIO (`gpiochip`, libgpiod), LEDs, vibrator, IR
    blaster, all **read-only by default**, with write access as an explicit per-node
    opt-in and a denylist for PMIC/charger/regulator nodes.
13. **Modem.** Report-only: modem type, interfaces, `rmnet` data interfaces usable for
    capture. No AT command or NV write path.

### Phase 4 — Linux GUI apps
Goal: open Wireshark from the Android launcher and get a usable window, with capture on
`wlan0`/`rmnet*`/USB adapters working for the user (dumpcap with the right
capabilities/groups on an Android kernel: verify how file capabilities interact with
`aid_net_raw`/`aid_net_admin`).
1. **Display server — research gate first** (write `docs/GUI.md`, then stop for my
   choice): compare (a) Termux:X11 as an external companion app, connected through a
   shared X socket in the chroot; (b) an X server or Wayland compositor **embedded in
   UbuRoot** rendering into a `SurfaceView` — with the GPL relicense, Termux:X11's code
   (GPL-3.0) is a candidate to embed or fork; also check Xwayland/wlroots licenses and
   arm64 build cost; (c) Xvnc/TigerVNC + an F-Droid VNC client (AVNC, bVNC) as the universal
   fallback. Criteria: latency, GPU path, per-app windows vs full desktop, external
   display (HDMI/USB-C DP, cast), maintenance, license.
2. **Modes.** *App mode*: one Linux app per Android window/task where the chosen
   display path supports it (rootless X or a per-app surface); *Desktop mode*: a full
   lightweight desktop (research XFCE vs LXQt on arm64 LTS) — the KeX equivalent. Bind
   to localhost only by default; LAN exposure is an explicit opt-in with a password.
3. **GPU acceleration.** Adreno: Mesa Turnip (Vulkan, KGSL backend) + Zink (OpenGL on
   Vulkan); verify the Mesa version and build flags Ubuntu ships and build/package our
   own only if needed. Mali and others: research (panfrost needs an upstream kernel
   driver Android kernels usually lack; virgl/venus through Android's driver; llvmpipe as
   the fallback). Report the active renderer (`glxinfo -B`, `vulkaninfo`) in the app.
4. **Audio** from Phase 3 wired into the session; **input**: touch as mouse with
   gestures (right click, scroll, drag), on-screen extra keys, hardware keyboard/mouse,
   IME text input, clipboard sync with Android (reuse `uburoot-clip`).
5. **Launcher integration.** Scan `/usr/share/applications/*.desktop` (and `~/.local`),
   list them in a *Linux apps* screen with their icons, and pin any of them as an Android
   launcher shortcut (`ShortcutManager`) that starts the display path + the app.
   `uburoot-open`/`xdg-open` keep working in both directions.
6. **Packaging pitfalls.** Ubuntu's `firefox`/`chromium` debs are snap transitions and
   snaps need systemd: detect and point to working alternatives (Mozilla's APT repo,
   Flatpak if it works without systemd — research). Same check for any app in the
   acceptance list.
7. **Acceptance apps** (tested on the device, results in `docs/GUI.md`): Wireshark (live
   capture), GIMP, a browser, gqrx with an RTL-SDR dongle (USB from Phase 3 + audio),
   Ghidra (Java), a code editor, glmark2/vkcube (GPU path).

### Phase 5 — native tool front-ends (NetHunter's Nmap Scan, generalized)
1. **Declarative tool definitions**: root-owned files in `/etc/uburoot/tools.d/*.json`
   (or TOML; pick one and document a schema with a version field): name, description,
   icon, category, `run_as` (user/root), **argv template** (an array, never a shell
   string), typed parameters (text, number, boolean, choice, file/dir picker via SAF,
   network interface/USB device/target pickers populated by a command), output mode
   (new terminal tab, streaming log, table via a parser — JSON/XML/CSV/regex —, or a
   result file opened with `uburoot-open` or a GUI app).
2. **Renderer**: a Compose screen that builds the form from the schema, validates input,
   shows the exact command before running, keeps a history and lets the user save
   presets and pin a tool as a launcher shortcut.
3. **Security**: only root-owned, non-writable definition files are loaded; parameters
   are passed as separate argv entries; root tools need a confirmation; schema
   validation with clear errors. Unit tests for the template expansion (injection
   attempts included).
4. **Bundled definitions** (examples, all defensive/diagnostic): nmap scan with XML →
   table, tcpdump capture to a pcap → open in Wireshark (Phase 4), iperf3, ping /
   traceroute / mtr, `iw` scan, apt update/upgrade, `uburoot-doctor`, `uburoot-hw`
   info, dmesg follow. A `docs/TOOLS.md` guide so the community can add more.

### Phase 6 — chroot manager
New "Ubuntu" management screen + scripts:
1. **Backup** (`backup.sh`): only with everything unmounted (`umount.sh --kill` first,
   abort if it fails) or with `--one-file-system` and an explicit exclude list of every
   bind target; `tar` with numeric owners, permissions and xattrs; compression choice;
   output to a SAF location; manifest with SHA256 and `/etc/uburoot-release`.
2. **Restore** (`restore.sh`): verify manifest and checksum, extract into
   `rootfs.partial`, atomic rename; the old rootfs is only removed through `ubu_safe_rm`.
3. **Verify** (`dpkg --verify`-based integrity report) and **remove** (existing reset flow).
4. **Move location** (e.g. to an ext4 adoptable/external partition), if the target
   filesystem supports ownership, device nodes are not needed and `suid` is possible;
   refuse FAT/exFAT/FUSE with a clear message.
5. **Profiles** (NetHunter's minimal/full, done the Ubuntu way): after install, optional
   package sets applied with `apt` (e.g. *minimal*, *development*, *network tools*,
   *desktop*, *hardware/SDR*, *kernel dev*). Lists live in a versioned file in assets,
   not hardcoded in Kotlin.
6. **Release upgrade** to the next LTS (`do-release-upgrade` or sources rewrite + full
   upgrade; research which one works without systemd), with a mandatory backup prompt.

### Phase 7 — services and custom commands
1. `uburoot-svc` (POSIX sh, no systemd): `start|stop|restart|status|enable|disable|logs
   NAME`, pidfiles in `/run/uburoot/`, logs in `/var/log/uburoot/`, definitions in
   `/etc/uburoot/services.d/NAME` (command, user, optional readiness check). `enable`
   integrates with the existing `/etc/uburoot-boot.d/` mechanism; do not create a second
   boot path. Ship examples: sshd, the desktop/display server (Phase 4), the audio and
   sensor bridges (Phase 3).
2. Services screen: list, status, start/stop, run-at-boot toggle, log tail.
3. Custom commands (NetHunter's "Custom Commands"): quick commands that run in a new tab
   or in the background with a result notification; pinnable as launcher shortcuts and
   as a Quick Settings tile. (Tools with parameters belong to Phase 5.)

### Phase 8 — status home screen
Replace the bare preflight-to-terminal flow with a home screen once installed: Ubuntu
state (mounted / not), sessions, services, network interfaces and addresses, disk usage
of the rootfs, kernel and hardware access state (what is granted/taken over, with
*Revert changes*), GPU renderer, quick actions (terminal, root terminal, Linux apps,
tools, stop all, repair mounts, doctor). Keep the current preflight logic underneath.

### Phase 9 — architectures and Android versions
1. Support `armhf` and `amd64` Ubuntu Base where the device ABI allows it
   (`ReleaseDiscovery`, `install.sh` arch check, `setup.sh` sources). Keep arm64 default.
2. Android 13–16: typed `startForeground` (API 34+), POST_NOTIFICATIONS (33+), any new
   restriction on `--mount-master`, background starts, broadcasts, camera/mic/sensor
   access from a background service (foreground-service types `camera`, `microphone`,
   etc. for the Phase 3 bridges). Verify in AOSP source.
3. CI: research running the device suite on a rootable x86_64 Android emulator image so
   amd64 and newer Android are exercised on every push.

### Phase 10 — kernel manager (NetHunter's "Kernel: search, download, flash")
Highest-risk phase. Nothing here runs on the test device without my explicit approval
each time.
1. **Backup first, always.** Identify the boot chain partitions of this device and slot
   (`boot`, `init_boot`, `vendor_boot`, `dtbo`, `vendor_dlkm`... via
   `/dev/block/by-name` and `ro.boot.slot_suffix`), dump them with SHA256 to a SAF
   location, and keep a manifest (device codename, build fingerprint, slot, kernel
   version). Flashing is refused without a verified backup of every partition it writes.
2. **Flash** a user-provided kernel: AnyKernel3 zips (verify how AK3 runs from a booted
   system) or raw images; check the device codename and partition sizes before writing;
   on A/B devices research flashing the inactive slot so the bootloader can fall back.
3. **Keep root.** A new boot/init_boot image loses Magisk/KernelSU/APatch: research and
   implement re-patching per manager (Magisk's boot patch script, KernelSU GKI/LKM,
   APatch's patcher), or stop with clear instructions.
4. **Restore** from a backup in the app, plus a documented fastboot/recovery path for
   when the phone no longer boots.
5. Kernel sources are only a file the user picks or a URL the user types; no catalog
   hosted by us.
6. Build guide (docs + optional CI recipe, not in-app): building a kernel for a device
   with extra options (USB gadget/HID, monitor-mode Wi-Fi drivers, CAN, v4l2loopback,
   BTF/kprobes, module signing off) from GKI or OEM source.

### Phase 11 — updates, settings backup, documentation
1. Optional update check against GitHub releases, in a separate build flavor that is
   **not** used for F-Droid (F-Droid updates the app itself). Off by default.
2. Export/import of app settings + `uburoot.conf` + services + custom commands + tool
   presets (NetHunter backs up `nh_files`).
3. Docs in `docs/` organised like NetHunter's: installing, first run, chroot manager,
   services, kernel access, hardware access, Linux GUI apps, tools, kernel manager,
   troubleshooting, compatibility, porting/testing checklist. Keep README short and link
   out. pt-BR versions where the README section already exists in English.

### Optional research — rootless edition (decision document only)
Evaluate a proot-based fallback for unrooted phones (NetHunter Rootless): what breaks
(mounts, raw sockets, iptables, boot, kernel and hardware access), performance,
maintenance cost, whether it belongs in this app or a separate one. Write
`docs/ROOTLESS.md`; implement nothing.

## Definition of done for every phase
- Scripts shellcheck-clean; Docker suite extended and green; unit tests for new Kotlin.
- Device test on the Samsung Galaxy S9 / LineageOS 19.1 / Magisk 31.1 when `adb devices`
  shows it; otherwise list precisely what is untested (and on which Android/root
  manager/SoC — the S9 is Snapdragon 845/Adreno 630, so Mali paths stay untested).
- Host mount table identical to the baseline after `umount.sh` (as the existing suites
  do), and every kernel/hardware change (tunable, module, SELinux, governor, takeover,
  gadget) back to the recorded original after *Revert changes* and after *Stop all*;
  Android's own Wi-Fi/Bluetooth/audio/camera work normally afterwards.
- `CLAUDE.md` (decisions + evidence), `README.md`, `CHANGELOG.md` (Unreleased),
  `docs/NETHUNTER_PARITY.md` (status column) updated.
    - Small Conventional Commits. Stop and show me the result before the next phase.

```
